Let me understand the data:
- train_X.csv: 200 rows, 3 columns (x1, x2, x3)
- train_y.csv: 200 rows (y column, one value per row)
- test_X.csv: 5 rows, 3 columns
- meta_public.json: P=3, jitter=1e-06

The R script:
1. Reads CSV with readr::read_csv (which returns tibbles/dataframes)
2. Converts train_X to matrix (200x3), test_X to matrix (5x3)
3. train_y <- train_y$y (vector of length 200)
4. P <- 3, jitter <- 1e-06
5. N <- 200, D <- 3, N_new <- 5
6. H <- cbind(1, train_X[,1], train_X[,2]) → 200x3 matrix (intercept + x1 + x2)
7. H_new <- cbind(1, test_X[,1], test_X[,2]) → 5x3 matrix

Interesting: H uses only columns 1 and 2 of X (not column 3). But D=3 for the GP covariance (uses all 3 columns).

Stan model:
- functions: cov_ard_sqexp, cross_cov_ard
- data: N, D, X (N×D), P, H (N×P), y (N), jitter, N_new, X_new (N_new×D), H_new (N_new×P)
- parameters: beta[P], alpha>0, rho[D]>0, sigma>0
- transformed parameters: mu = H*beta; K = cov_ard_sqexp + diag(sigma^2); L_K = cholesky_decompose(K)
- model: beta ~ normal(0,1), alpha ~ normal(0, 1.5), rho ~ lognormal(0,1), sigma ~ normal(0, 0.5), y ~ multi_normal_cholesky(mu, L_K)
- generated quantities: y_new predictions with posterior predictive sampling

Sampling hyperparameters:
- chains = 4, iter = 2000, warmup = 1000, thin = 2 → number of post-warmup samples per chain = (2000-1000)/2 = 500, 4 chains → 2000 posterior samples
- seed = 1
- control: adapt_delta=0.93, max_treedepth=14, adapt_gamma=0.05, adapt_kappa=0.75, adapt_t0=10, adapt_init_buffer=100, adapt_term_buffer=50, adapt_window=25
- init_r = 0.1
- save_warmup = FALSE
- refresh = 200

I need to convert this to PyStan 3.10.0. All the control parameters should be supported in PyStan.

- `stan.build(program_code, data=data, random_seed=1)` 
- `stan.sample(model, data=data, ...)` for sampling

import stan
program_code = "..."

```

import stan

```python
def sample(
    self,
    data=None,
    *,
    num_chains=1,
    num_samples=1000,

    save_warmup=False,
    thin=1,
    seed=None,
    init=None,

    adapt_term_buffer=None,
    adapt_window=None,
    fixed_param=False,
    show_console=True,
    refresh=None,
    include_extra=False,
)
```

The R sampling:
- chains=4, iter=2000, warmup=1000, thin=2
- In Stan, iter includes warmup. So n_post_samples_per_chain = (iter - warmup)/thin = (2000-1000)/2 = 500. Total 2000 samples.

In PyStan 3, `num_samples` corresponds to samples after warmup, and `num_warmup` is warmup iterations. There's no `iter` param directly; you specify num_samples and num_warmup. So to replicate: num_chains=4, num_samples=500, num_warmup=1000, thin=2.

In PyStan 3, `num_samples` is the number of samples to draw AFTER warmup, unthinned (i.e., before thinning?). Actually let me recall: In PyStan 3, `num_samples` is "Number of samples to draw" after warmup. Then `thin` thins them. So num_samples=500, thin=2 means 250 retained?

Hmm, this is ambiguous. Let me check PyStan's behavior carefully Maz.

Actually from pystan 3 docs:

```
num_samples: Number of samples to draw after warm-up. Defaults to 1000.
num_warmup: Number of warmup iterations. Defaults to 1000.
thin: Period between saved samples. Defaults to 1.
```

So in PyStan, num_samples already represents total iterations after warmup. Then the number of saved samples = ceil(num_samples / thin)? Or is num_samples the number of saved?

Let me look at how pystan3 maps to cmdstanpy.

PyStan 3.10 is built on cmdstanpy. The `sample` method... Actually PyStan 3 uses its own interface with cmdstanpy internally newsletter. Let me recall.

Actually PyStan 3.10: `stan.build` returns a StanModel (or something) and its `sample` method calls cmdstanpy's `CmdStanModel.sample` under the hood. In cmdstanpy:

```python
model.sample(
    data=..., 
    chains=4,
    iter_warmup=1000,
    iter_sampling=500,
    thin=2,
    ...
)
```

`iter_sampling` is iterations in the sampling phase (before thinning). So num_saved = iter_sampling / thin = 250 per chain in that case.

[tool-f15] read_file ->     31	    """Calculate model name from Stan program code.
[tool-f15] read_file ->     32	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     33	    Names look like this: ``models/2uxewutp``. Name uses a hash of the
[tool-f15] read_file ->     34	    concatenation of the following:
[tool-f15] read_file ->     35	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     36	    - UTF-8 encoded Stan program code
[tool-f15] read_file ->     37	    - UTF-8 encoded string recording the httpstan version
[tool-f15] read_file ->     38	    - UTF-8 encoded string identifying the system platform
[tool-f15] read_file ->     39	    - UTF-8 encoded string identifying the system bit architecture
[tool-f15] read_file ->     40	    - UTF-8 encoded string identifying the Python version
[tool-f15] read_file ->     41	    - UTF-8 encoded string identifying the Python executable
[tool-f15] read_file ->     42	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     43	    Arguments:
[tool-f15] read_file ->     44	        program_code: Stan program code.
[tool-f15] read_file ->     45	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     46	    Returns:
[tool-f15] read_file ->     47	        str: model name
[tool-f15] read_file ->     48	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     49	    """
[tool-f15] read_file ->     50	    # digest_size of 5 means we expect a collision after a million models
[tool-f15] read_file ->     51	    digest_size = 5
[tool-f15] read_file ->     52	    hash = hashlib.blake2b(digest_size=digest_size)
[tool-f15] read_file ->     53	    hash.update(program_code.encode())
[tool-f15] read_file ->     54	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     55	    # system identifiers
[tool-f15] read_file ->     56	    hash.update(httpstan.__version__.encode())
[tool-f15] read_file ->     57	    hash.update(sys.platform.encode())
[tool-f15] read_file ->     58	    hash.update(str(sys.maxsize).encode())
[tool-f15] read_file ->     59	    hash.update(sys.version.encode())

[tool-f15] read_file ->     61	    hash.update(sys.executable.encode())
[tool-f15] read_file ->     62	
[tool-f15] read_file ->     63	    id = base64.b32encode(hash.digest()).decode().lower()
[tool-f15] read_file ->     64	    return f"models/{id}"

So httpstan needs a C++ compiler at runtime to compile each model (setuptools Extension build). And it needs the precompiled `stan_services.o` plus libs (sundials, tbb) plus headers (stan, stan/math, boost, eigen, sundials, tbb, pybind11, rapidjson) and the `stanc` compiler to convert Stan to C++.

Building all this from source on aarch64 is possible but very heavy — need to download ~large archives (stan, math, boost, eigen, sundials, tbb, pybind11, rapidjson) and build sundials + TBB. It could take a long time and lots of disk, but might work. However, we're missing a C++ compiler. We could `apt install build-essential`. But httpstan wheel already contains all these libraries compiled for x86_64; building them for aarch64 from source would require the full Stan Math build.

This is an enormous effort. Let me reconsider if there's a simpler approach.

We have g++ 13.3.0, make 4.3, 20 cores, lots of RAM and disk. 

Now, let me build httpstan 4.13.0 from source. Steps:

1. Get the httpstan 4.13.0 source (already at /tmp/httpstan-4.13.0).
2. Modify the Makefile to use `linux-arm64-stanc` on Linux arm64. The Makefile currently downloads `linux-stanc` (x86_64). Let me update the Makefile to conditionally download the ARM binary. Actually simplest: modify the `build/stanc:` rule to curl the correct binary based on `uname -m`. Or manually download the arm64 stanc into build/stanc before running make — but make's rule will overwrite it. Better to patch the Makefile.

Let me update the rule:
```
ifeq ($(shell uname -s),Darwin)
build/stanc:
	curl --location https://github.com/stan-dev/stanc3/releases/download/v$(STANC_VERSION)/mac-stanc -o $@ --retry 5 --fail
else ifeq ($(shell uname -m),aarch64)
build/stanc:
	curl --location https://github.com/stan-dev/stanc3/releases/download/v$(STANC_VERSION)/linux-arm64-stanc -o $@ --retry 5 --fail
else
build/stanc:
	curl --location https://github.com/stan-dev/stanc3/releases/download/v$(STANC_VERSION)/linux-stanc -o $@ --retry 5 --fail
endif
```

Also, in Makefile.build (setup), `empty.cpp` compiles with pybind11 include. pybind11 headers are architecture-independent. Fine.

3. Run `make -j$(nproc) default` in the httpstan source. This downloads archives from GitHub, extracts, builds sundials/tbb, copies headers, copies stanc, compiles stan_services.o.

Concerns:
- Stan 2.35.0 download: ~few MB of source (stan src).
- Math 4.9.0: ~large (includes boost, eigen, sundials, tbb). Could be ~200MB.
- Building sundials & TBB: TBB 2020.3 compilation on modern gcc might have warnings but should workley. Stan Math's makefile handles building these.

4. Then `python3 -m pip install --break-system-packages .` in the source to install httpstan (it builds the empty extension and packages the libs/headers/stanc/stan_services.o into site-packages via the `include` in pyproject).

Wait — pyproject.toml includes only some files: `httpstan/*.o`, `httpstan/*.cpp`, `httpstan/lib/libsundials*`, `httpstan/lib/libtbb*`, `httpstan/stanc`, `httpstan/include/**/*`, `doc/openapi.yaml`. So after `make`, all these files should be in place and will be packaged into the wheel by poetry-core.

However, `python -m pip install .` uses poetry-core as build backend. Do we have poetry-core installed? pip will install build dependencies (setuptools, poetry-core) automatically when building from source if network available.

Let me verify the pyproject [build-system] requires: ["setuptools", "poetry-core>=1.0.0"].

Then `pip install --break-system-packages /tmp/httpstan-4.13.0`.

5. Then install pystan 3.10.0 and its other deps (aiohttp, clikit, numpy, pysimdjson, setuptools). These are already partially available? Let me check currently installed packages.

PyStan builds models (compilation works! 10.2s for tiny model). But `seed` is not a valid kwarg for sample() in this version — it seems the sample method already receives seed from the model/random_seed. Let me inspect the pystan model.py sample signature to understand valid kwargs.

[tool-2bb] read_file ->    129	    def _create_fit(self, *, function, num_chains, **kwargs) -> stan.fit.Fit:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    130	        """Make a request to httpstan's ``create_fit`` endpoint and process results.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    131	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    132	        Users should not use this function.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    133	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    134	        Parameters in ``kwargs`` will be passed to the (Python wrapper of)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    135	        `function`. Parameter names are identical to those used in CmdStan.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    136	        See the CmdStan documentation for parameter descriptions and default
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    137	        values.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    138	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    139	        Returns:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    140	            Fit: instance of Fit allowing access to draws.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    141	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    142	        """
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    143	        assert "chain" not in kwargs, "`chain` id is set automatically."
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    144	        assert "data" not in kwargs, "`data` is set in `build`."
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    145	        assert "random_seed" not in kwargs, "`random_seed` is set in `build`."
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    146	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    147	        # copy kwargs and verify everything is JSON-encodable
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    148	        kwargs = json.loads(DataJSONEncoder().encode(kwargs))
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    149	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    150	        # FIXME: special handling here for `init`, consistent with PyStan 2 but needs docs
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    151	        init: List[Data] = kwargs.pop("init", [dict() for _ in range(num_chains)])
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    152	        if len(init) != num_chains:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    153	            raise ValueError("Initial values must be provided for each chain.")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    154	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    155	        payloads = []
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    156	        for chain in range(1, num_chains + 1):
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    157	            payload = kwargs.copy()
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    158	            payload["function"] = function
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    159	            payload["chain"] = chain  # type: ignore
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    160	            payload["data"] = self.data  # type: ignore
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    161	            payload["init"] = init.pop(0)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    162	            if self.random_seed is not None:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    163	                payload["random_seed"] = self.random_seed  # type: ignore
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    164	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    165	            # fit needs to know num_samples, num_warmup, num_thin, save_warmup
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    166	            # progress reporting needs to know some of these
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    167	            num_warmup = payload.get("num_warmup", arguments.lookup_default(arguments.Method["SAMPLE"], "num_warmup"))
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    168	            num_samples = payload.get(
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    169	                "num_samples",
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    170	                arguments.lookup_default(arguments.Method["SAMPLE"], "num_samples"),
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    171	            )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    172	            num_thin = payload.get("num_thin", arguments.lookup_default(arguments.Method["SAMPLE"], "num_thin"))
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    173	            save_warmup = payload.get(
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    174	                "save_warmup",
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    175	                arguments.lookup_default(arguments.Method["SAMPLE"], "save_warmup"),
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    176	            )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    177	            payloads.append(payload)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    178	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    179	        async def go():
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    180	            io = ConsoleIO()
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    181	            sampling_output = io.section().error_output
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    182	            percent_complete = 0
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    183	            sampling_output.write_line(f"<comment>Sampling:</comment> {percent_complete:3.0f}%")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    184	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    185	            current_and_max_iterations_re = re.compile(r"Iteration:\s+(\d+)\s+/\s+(\d+)")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    186	            async with stan.common.HttpstanClient() as client:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    187	                operations = []
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    188	                for payload in payloads:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    189	                    resp = await client.post(f"/{self.model_name}/fits", json=payload)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    190	                    if resp.status == 422:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    191	                        raise ValueError(str(resp.json()))
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    192	                    elif resp.status != 201:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    193	                        raise RuntimeError(resp.json()["message"])
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    194	                    assert resp.status == 201
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    195	                    operations.append(resp.json())
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    196	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    197	                # poll to get progress for each chain until all chains finished
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    198	                current_iterations = {}
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    199	                while not all(operation["done"] for operation in operations):
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    200	                    for operation in operations:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    201	                        if operation["done"]:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    202	                            continue
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    203	                        resp = await client.get(f"/{operation['name']}")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    204	                        assert resp.status != 404
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    205	                        operation.update(resp.json())
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    206	                        progress_message = operation["metadata"].get("progress")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    207	                        if not progress_message:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    208	                            continue
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    209	                        iteration, iteration_max = map(
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    210	                            int, current_and_max_iterations_re.findall(progress_message).pop(0)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    211	                        )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    212	                        if current_iterations.get(operation["name"]) == iteration:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    213	                            continue
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    214	                        current_iterations[operation["name"]] = iteration
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    215	                        iterations_count = sum(current_iterations.values())
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    216	                        total_iterations = iteration_max * num_chains
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    217	                        percent_complete = 100 * iterations_count / total_iterations
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    218	                        sampling_output.clear() if io.supports_ansi() else sampling_output.write("\n")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    219	                        sampling_output.write_line(
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    220	                            f"<comment>Sampling:</comment> {round(percent_complete):3.0f}% ({iterations_count}/{total_iterations})"
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    221	                        )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    222	                    await asyncio.sleep(0.05)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    223	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    224	                fit_in_cache = len(current_iterations) < num_chains
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    225	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    226	                stan_outputs = []
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    227	                for operation in operations:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    228	                    fit_name = operation["result"].get("name")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    229	                    if fit_name is None:  # operation["result"] is an error
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    230	                        assert not str(operation["result"]["code"]).startswith("2"), operation
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    231	                        message = operation["result"]["message"]
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    232	                        if """ValueError('Initialization failed.')""" in message:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    233	                            sampling_output.clear()
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    234	                            sampling_output.write_line("<info>Sampling:</info> <error>Initialization failed.</error>")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    235	                            raise RuntimeError("Initialization failed.")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    236	                        raise RuntimeError(message)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    237	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    238	                    resp = await client.get(f"/{fit_name}")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    239	                    if resp.status != 200:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    240	                        raise RuntimeError((resp.json())["message"])
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    241	                    stan_outputs.append(resp.content)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    242	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    243	                    # clean up after ourselves when fit is uncacheable (no random seed)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    244	                    if self.random_seed is None:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    245	                        resp = await client.delete(f"/{fit_name}")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    246	                        if resp.status not in {200, 202, 204}:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    247	                            raise RuntimeError((resp.json())["message"])
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    248	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    249	                sampling_output.clear() if io.supports_ansi() else sampling_output.write("\n")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    250	                sampling_output.write_line(
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    251	                    "<info>Sampling:</info> 100%, done."
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    252	                    if fit_in_cache
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    253	                    else f"<info>Sampling:</info> {percent_complete:3.0f}% ({iterations_count}/{total_iterations}), done."
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    254	                )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    255	                if not io.supports_ansi():
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    256	                    sampling_output.write("\n")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    257	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    258	            stan_outputs = tuple(stan_outputs)  # Fit constructor expects a tuple.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    259	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    260	            def is_nonempty_logger_message(msg: simdjson.Object):
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    261	                return msg["topic"] == "logger" and msg["values"][0] != "info:"  # type: ignore
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    262	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    263	            def is_iteration_or_elapsed_time_logger_message(msg: simdjson.Object):
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    264	                # Assumes `msg` is a message with topic `logger`.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    265	                text = msg["values"][0]  # type: ignore
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    266	                text = cast(str, text)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    267	                return (
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    268	                    text.startswith("info:Iteration:")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    269	                    or text.startswith("info: Elapsed Time:")
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    270	                    # this detects lines following "Elapsed Time:", part of a multi-line Stan message
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    271	                    or text.startswith("info:" + " " * 15)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    272	                )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    273	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    274	            parser = simdjson.Parser()
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    275	            nonstandard_logger_messages = []
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    276	            for stan_output in stan_outputs:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    277	                for line in stan_output.splitlines():
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    278	                    # Do not attempt to parse non-logger messages. Draws could contain nan or inf values.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    279	                    # simdjson cannot parse lines containing such values.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    280	                    if b'"logger"' not in line:
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    281	                        continue
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    282	                    msg = parser.parse(line)
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    283	                    if is_nonempty_logger_message(msg) and not is_iteration_or_elapsed_time_logger_message(msg):
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    284	                        nonstandard_logger_messages.append(msg.as_dict())
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    285	                    del msg
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    286	            del parser  # simdjson.Parser is no longer used at this point.
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    287	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    288	            if nonstandard_logger_messages:

[tool-2bb] read_file ->    290	                for msg in nonstandard_logger_messages:

[tool-2bb] read_file ->    292	                    if text.strip():

[tool-2bb] read_file ->    294	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    295	            fit = stan.fit.Fit(

[tool-2bb] read_file ->    297	                num_chains,

[tool-2bb] read_file ->    305	            )
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    306	
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    307	            for entry_point in stan.plugins.get_plugins():
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    308	                Plugin = entry_point.load()
[tool-2bb] read_file ->    309	                fit = Plugin().on_post_sample(fit)

Now I understand PyStan 3.10 APIs:

- `stan.build(program_code, data, random_seed=1)` — random_seed is set at build timeable. It's passed to `sample` via `self.random_seed` in the payload. So each chain gets a random_seed.
- `posterior.sample(num_chains=4, num_samples=..., num_warmup=..., num_thin=..., save_warmup=..., ...)` — kwargs are those from CmdStan's hmc_nuts_diag_e_adapt. `seed` is NOT valid (it comes from build's random_seed).

Let me look at the `arguments.py` in httpstan services to see the supported arguments (names and defaults) and how `num_samples`, `num_warmup`, `num_thin` map to the underlying Stan services.

RStan's sampling: chains=4, iter=2000, warmup=1000, thin=2.

In Stan services (the underlying C++), the arguments are `num_samples`, `num_warmup`, `num_thin`. In RStan, `iter` = num_samples + num_warmup in the services sense.

For RStan: iter=2000, warmup=1000 → num_warmup=1000, num_samples=1000. thin=2 → num_thin=2. This yields (2000-1000)/2 = 500 draws per chain, total 2000.

In PyStan 3, we call `posterior.sample(num_chains=4, num_warmup=1000, num_samples=1000, num_thin=2, ...)`.

Note the Fit uses num_thin to compute the number of draws per chain: draws = num_samples // num_thin = 1000//2 = 500. Let me verify in fit.py how theta/sample counts are determined.

Let me look at the httpstan arguments defaults to confirm exact parameter names: adapt_delta, max_treedepth, adapt_gamma, adapt_kappa, adapt_t0, adapt_init_buffer, adapt_term_buffer, adapt_window, init_r, save_warmup, refresh? Hmm — `refresh` might not be a valid argument in PyStan 3. In RStan `refresh` controls console output. In PyStan 3, progress reporting is handled by the pystan client, not by a `refresh` control argument. The httpstan arguments may accept `refresh`? Let me check httpstan/services/arguments.py to see defaults and supported params.

[tool-a5b] read_file ->    141	class WriterMessage(marshmallow.Schema):
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    142	    """Messages from callback writers and loggers in ``stan::callbacks``.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    143	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    144	    NOTE: You SHOULD NOT use this schema. This schema exists for testing and

[tool-a5b] read_file ->    147

[tool-a5b] read_file ->    149	    from
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    150	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    151	    - ``stan/callbacks/writer.hpp``, and
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    152	    - ``stan/callbacks/logger.hpp``.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    153	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    154	    In particular, the schema matches a JSON-based "version" of the CSV-focused
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    155	    ``stan/callbacks/stream_writer.hpp`` and
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    156	    ``stan/callbacks/stream_logger.hpp``.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    157	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    158	    This version is found "inside" the httpstan-specific
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    159	    ``httpstan/socket_writer.hpp`` and ``httpstan/socket_logger.hpp``.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    160	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    161	    `WriterMessage` is a data format for all messages written by the callback
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    162	    writers defined in ``stan::callbacks``.  These writers are used by the
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    163	    functions defined in ``stan::services``. For example,
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    164	    ``stan::services::sample::hmc_nuts_diag_e`` uses one logger and three
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    165	    writers:
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    166	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    167	    - ``logger`` Logger for informational and error messages
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    168	    - ``init_writer`` Writer callback for unconstrained inits
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    169	    - ``sample_writer`` Writer for draws
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    170	    - ``diagnostic_writer`` Writer for diagnostic information
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    171	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    172	    WriterMessage is a format which is flexible enough to accommodate these
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    173	    different uses while still providing a predictable structure.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    174	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    175	    A WriterMessage has a field ``topic`` which provides information about what
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    176	    the WriterMessage concerns or what produced it. For example, the `topic`
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    177	    associated with a WriterMessage written by `sample_writer` in the function
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    178	    is ``sample``.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    179	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    180	    The "content" of a message is stored in the field ``values``. This is either
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    181	    a list or a mapping.
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    182	
[tool-a5b] read_file ->    183	    """

Now I understand the valid kwargs for PyStan 3.10's sample() (which gets passed to CreateFitRequest):
- `num_warmup`, `num_samples`, `num_thin` (not `thin` — hmm, wait; in the arguments module `num_thin` is special-handled with arg="thin", but the schemas use `num_thin`), `save_warmup`, `init_radius`, `refresh`, `stepsize`, `stepsize_jitter`, `max_depth`, `delta`, `gamma`, `kappa`, `t0`, `init_buffer`, `term_buffer`, `window`. Also `init` (list of dicts, special-handled).

Note: `num_thin` is the schema field, so I'll pass `num_thin=2`.

Also `random_seed` is set from `stan.build(..., random_seed=1)`; each chain gets the same value. RStan with seed=1 uses the same for all chains — identical behavior. 

Now, `init_r` in RStan corresponds to `init_radius` in PyStan 3 (CmdStan). The R script uses `init_r = 0.1`. We'll pass `init_radius=0.1`.

Now let me understand how `num_samples` and `num_thin` affect the number of draws in the Fit. In the underlying httpstan/stan_services.cpp wrapper, the hmc_nuts_diag_e_adapt_wrapper signature (from compile warning):

This confirms the mapping exactly:
- `init_radius` ↔ RStan's `init_r`
- `num_warmup` ↔ RStan's `warmup`
- `num_samples` ↔ RStan's `iter - warmup` = 1000
- `num_thin` ↔ RStan's `thin` = 2
- `save_warmup` ↔ FALSE
- `refresh` — RStan's refresh=200 (used for progress reporting)
- `stepsize`, `stepsize_jitter` — default (not used in RStan)
- `max_depth` ↔ `max_treedepth` = 14
- `delta` ↔ `adapt_delta` = 0.93
- `gamma` ↔ `adapt_gamma` = 0.05
- `kappa` ↔ `adapt_kappa` = 0.75
- `t0` ↔ `adapt_t0` = 10
- `init_buffer` ↔ `adapt_init_buffer` = 100
- `term_buffer` ↔ `adapt_term_buffer` = 50
- `window` ↔ `adapt_window` = 25

Great. So the PyStan sample call will be:

```python
fit = posterior.sample(
    num_chains=4,
    num_warmup=1000,
    num_samples=1000,
    num_thin=2,
    save_warmup=False,
    init_radius=0.1,
    refresh=200,
    max_depth=14,
    delta=0.93,
    gamma=0.05,
    kappa=0.75,
    t0=10,
    init_buffer=100,
    term_buffer=50,
    window=25,
)
```

And `stan.build(program_code, data=data, random_seed=1)`.

Since `random_seed` is set in build, each chain is given the same seed=1 (matching RStan `seed=1`).

Initial values: RStan `init_r=0.1` uses random initial values drawn uniformly in (-init_r, init_r) in unconstrained space. PyStan's init_radius=0.1 does the same. Good.

Note: In PyStan, if we don't pass `init`, it defaults to `[dict() for _ in range(num_chains)]` (empty init dicts → random init). Good.

Now let me write the Python script `/app/pystan_analysis.py`, then run it.

Let me carefully translate the R script:

**Data loading**
- `train_X.csv`: matrix 200×3 (columns x1,x2,x3), read with readr::read_csv which reads as tibble (column-major). `as.matrix` converts. Column order preserved: x1, x2, x3.
- `test_X.csv`: 5×3 matrix.
- `train_y.csv`: has a column `y`; `train_y$y` extracts vector of length 200.
- `meta_public.json`: P=3, jitter=1e-06.

**Data construction**
- N=200, D=3, N_new=5
- H <- cbind(1, train_X[,1], train_X[,2]) — a 200×3 matrix: [1, x1, x2]
- H_new <- cbind(1, test_X[,1], test_X[,2]) — 5×3 matrix
- data_list = {N, D, X=train_X, P, H, y=train_y, jitter, N_new, X_new=test_X, H_new}

Wait — in RStan, a matrix[N,D] X data is passed as a 2D array. In PyStan, data is passed as a nested list (list of lists) with shape. PyStan 3's data encoder converts numpy arrays to nested lists. Stan infers matrix dimensions from data: a nested list of N lists of D numbers is interpreted as matrix[N,D] (row-major order). Python's nested lists are row-major, matching Stan's matrix indexing (first index row). And R's `as.matrix` stored in column-major, but since we convert to Python nested lists where each row is one observation, it matches matrix[N,D] with X[i,d]. Good.

**Stan model code**: I should translate it function-equivalently. The Stan code can be reused as-is (it's the same language). The task says "your converted Stan model code is functionally equivalent to the original stan model in R script (optional: optimize the Stan model for memory efficiency)".

I can keep the same Stan code but optimize memory: instead of computing full matrix K and its Cholesky in transformed parameters and storing, I could compute log density directly using multi_normal_cholesky_lpdf. Actually the standard optimization is to compute the log likelihood via Cholesky factorization within the model block using `multi_normal_cholesky_lpdf`. But we also need L_K in generated quantities (for prediction). Hmm, for functional equivalence we can keep transformed parameters but that's fine for N=200.

Actually the R stan model stores K (200×200), L_K (200×200). That's fine memory-wise (200×200 doubles = 320KB). Not a problemniert.

However, "optimize for memory efficiency" is optional. I'll keep it functionally equivalent but perhaps simpler. Let me keep essentially the same Stan code verbatim to guarantee equivalence, since it's the same Stan language. I'll maybe just add the standard optimization of computing the log likelihood with Cholesky in the model:

```stan
model {
  ...
  y ~ multi_normal_cholesky(H * beta, L_K);
}
```

That's exactly what the original does. Actually with multi_normal_cholesky, the arguments are mu vector and Cholesky factor L. The original computes L_K = cholesky_decompose(K) in the transformed parameters and then uses multi_normal_cholesky(mu, L_K). Same thing.

I'll keep the code essentially identical to guarantee the same numerical behavior / sampling. One subtlety: The order of operations and floating point exactly determine the MCMC output. Since we must produce the same posterior sampling "Given the same data... exactly the same posterior sampling as the original R script", we should use the identical Stan program text (byte-for-byte ideally) so that the model hash/compilation matches.

Wait, but the constraint says "your converted Stan model code is functionally equivalent to the original stan model in R script (optional: optimize the Stan model for memory efficiency)". Since Stan code is the same language, I can literally copy the same Stan code. That guarantees functional equivalence and even identical compilation output.

Important nuance: RStan and PyStan use the same Stan C++ (2.35.0). The model code compiled by stanc v2.35.0 → deterministic sampling with seed 1 should be identical given identical code & data & hyperparameters & random initialization scheme & platform? Sampling on different platforms might differ slightly due to floating point (ARM vs x86). But that's fine; can't control.

The requirement "should do exactly the same posterior sampling as the original R script" means the sampling configuration should match: chains=4, iter/warmup/thin, seed, adapt params, init_r. We match all.

Now about `refresh`: RStan refresh=200 is just output frequency, doesn't change sampling. pass refresh=200.

One more important nuance: In RStan, `init_r = 0.1` with `control` not containing `init` — random initialization. PyStan: init_radius=0.1, and `init` defaults to list of empty dicts. Does the random initialization use the same RNG stream as RStan? Given random_seed=1, Stan uses the seed + chain to generate the initial values. In RStan, the RNG for init values also derives from seed & chain. In CmdStan/httpstan, the same seed & chain is used. So the init values should be the same if the underlying code is the same. Good.

Now let me confirm the `train_y` CSV: header row "y" then values. readr::read_csv("train_y.csv") gives tibble with column y. `train_y$y` extracts vector. So y = the single column values.

Let me also double check H construction: `H <- cbind(1, train_X[,1], train_X[,2])`. In R, if train_X is 200×3, then train_X[,1] is a vector of length 200cars. cbind gives a 200×3 matrix: first column all 1, second column = x1, third = x2. So H is [1, x1, x2]. Similarly H_new is [1, test_x1, test_x2].

Now, about matrix vs row-vector in Stan data: Stan's matrix[N,D] in R shares data as column-major arrays. In Python, nested lists are row-major. Stan's internal representation of matrix data reads values as row-major (X[i,d] with i=1..N, d=1..D). Since both R list conversion and Python list conversion send the same rows, it should be equivalent. In RStan's Python conversion, the nested list is converted to the same. For a matrix (not array), the R data_list$X = as.matrix(200×3) gets sent to RStan as a matrix with column-major storage but Stan reads matrix data in row-major from the 1D array json. RStan handles the conversion: a matrix in R is column-major, and rstan sends it with the proper ordering so that X[i,j] is correct. Since R's matrix has the same values (column-major storage doesn't change the logical matrix X[i,j]), and Python nested lists represent X[i][j] rows, the logical content is identical: X[i,1], X[i,2], X[i,3] are the i-th row values. Good.

The GP model (fuxgpnlm) fits directory is empty — meaning the error happened before any chain finished and cached its fit. The error at 50% suggests maybe the process was interrupted by exception in services_stub during call (the logger message float issue), probably when one chain produced a numeric logger value.

Wait actually, re-reading the traceback more carefully:

```
RuntimeError: Exception during call to services function: `AttributeError("'float' object has no attribute 'startswith'")`, traceback: `['  File "/usr/local/lib/python3.12/dist-packages/httpstan/services_stub.py", line 175, in call\n    if logger_message.startswith("warn:"):...'
```

This traceback comes from model.py line 236: `raise RuntimeError(message)` where `message = operation["result"]["message"]`. So the services function raised an exception during the call: the exception is `AttributeError('float' object has no attribute 'startswith')` originating in services_stub.py line 175 — that's in the error handling block (error_code != 0 path). ```

```

```

Wait, in the original R stan code:

```
matrix[N, N] v = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew);
```

Hmm — that looks wrong: `K_x_xnew` is [N, N_new] (200×5), so `mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew)` produces an [N, N_new] matrix, NOT [N,N]. But the original R script declares `matrix[N, N] v`, which would be a dimension mismatch... but the R script is claimed to work? Wait, let me re-read the R script:

```
matrix[N, N] v = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew);
vector[N_new] f_var = to_vector(diagonal(K_new)) - to_vector(columns_dot_self(v));
```

Hmm, `v` should be matrix[N, N_new] (i.e., each column of v corresponds to the solution for one new point). `columns_dot_self(v)` computes the sum of squares of each column — with v being [N, N_new], gives N_new values. So the R script declaring `matrix[N, N] v` is a BUG in the original script!

Wait — but the task says the R script is the reference and should produce results. Let me re-check the original R script text:

```
matrix[N, N_new] K_x_xnew = cross_cov_ard(X, X_new, rho, alpha);

vector[N] resid = y - (H * beta);

alpha_vec = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K', alpha_vec);

matrix[N, N_new] v = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew);

Actually Stan's type checking would flag `matrix[N,N]` vs expression return `[N, N_new]` — that's a static dimension mismatch, which stanc SHOULD catch...

Wait, in Stan, generated quantities are evaluated for each post-warmup iteration. The task says: "your converted Stan model code is functionally equivalent to the original stan model in R script (optional: optimize the Stan model for memory efficiency) — Given the same data, your converted script should do exactly the same posterior sampling as the original R script".

Since the original R script can't complete (the generated quantities would throw), we need to produce posterior means. The clear resolution: the R script has a typo `matrix[N, N] v` that should be `matrix[N, N_new] v` to make the GP posterior predictive work. This is the obvious fix — the standard GP posterior predictive formula uses K_x_xnew_* (N×N_new), and mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew) → (N, N_new) matrix; columns_dot_self(v) sums over N rows per column, giving N_new values — consistent with f_var length N_new.

So I'll fix the Stan model in my script to use `matrix[N, N_new] v`.

Actually wait — let me double check whether stanc would reject `matrix[N,N] v = ...` at compile... it didn't; the runtime check caught it. The R script has the same typo and would fail at runtime in RStan too. So the "original" R script is broken. The task intends us to write a working converted script that does the same posterior sampling (parameters sampling part). The generated quantities block is basically predictive draws; the parameters (beta, alpha, sigma, rho) are sampled in the model block, which is unaffected. The posterior means of alpha, sigma, rho, beta only depend on the sampling phase (model block), NOT on generated quantities. But Stan evaluates GQs after warmup for each sample; if GQ throws, the whole chain fails.

So to get posterior samples, we MUST fix the GQ bug. This is the right move: fix `matrix[N, N] v` → `matrix[N, N_new] v`. This is the minimal, obviously-intended fix that makes the model work and produces the correct posterior predictive. The parameter inference (alpha/sigma/rho/beta) is unaffected by this fix, so "same posterior sampling" for the parameters is preserved.

Let me update the Stan code in /app/pystan_analysis.py.

Wait, but is there another subtlety? Let me look at the covariance in GQ:

```
matrix[N, N_new] K_x_xnew = cross_cov_ard(X, X_new, rho, alpha);   // [N, N_new]
matrix[N_new, N_new] K_new = cov_ard_sqexp(X_new, rho, alpha, jitter);  // [N_new, N_new] with jitter
vector[N] resid = y - (H * beta);
vector[N] alpha_vec = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, resid_);  // solves L a = resid
alpha_vec = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K', alpha_vec);        // solves L' a = ... => K^{-1} resid
vector[N_new] f_mean = (K_x_xnew' * alpha_vec);           // [N_new, N]
matrix[N, N_new] v = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew);  // [N, M]
vector[N_new] f_var = to_vector(diagonal(K_new)) - to_vector(columns_dot_self(v));
// Diagonal of K_new is alpha^2 + jitter + sigma^2? 

Wait, K_new = cov_ard_sqexp(X_new, ...) + off diag? No — `cov_ard_sqexp` includes jitter on the diagonal and alpha^2 diag. But the predictive variance should also include the noise sigma^2? The f_var they compute is the GP predictive variance (latent function), then m_sd = sqrt(f_var + sigma^2) includes observation noise. Actually, `K_new = cov_ard_sqexp(X_new, rho, alpha, jitter)` includes jitter (=1e-6) on the diagonal, so f_var includes that tiny jitter. Fine.
```

So with N=200, N_new=5, `v` should be matrix[N, N_new] = [200, 5]. Fix is correct.

[tool-d40] read_file ->     19	functions {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     20	  matrix cov_ard_sqexp(matrix X, vector rho, real alpha, real jitter) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     21	    int N = rows(X);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     22	    int D = cols(X);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     23	    matrix[N, N] K;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     24	
[tool-d40] read_file ->     25	    for (i in 1:N) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     26	      K[i, i] = square(alpha) + jitter;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     27	      for (j in (i + 1):N) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     28	        real s = 0;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     29	        for (d in 1:D) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     30	          real t = (X[i, d] - X[j, d]) / rho[d];
[tool-d40] read_file ->     31	          s += t * t;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     32	        }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     33	        K[i, j] = square(alpha) * exp(-0.5 * s);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     34	        K[j, i] = K[i, j];
[tool-d40] read_file ->     35	      }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     36	    }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     37	    return K;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     38	  }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     39	
[tool-d40] read_file ->     40	  matrix cross_cov_ard(matrix X, matrix X_new, vector rho, real alpha) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     41	    int N = rows(X);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     42	    int M = rows(X_new);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     43	    int D = cols(X); // == cols(X_new)

[tool-d40] read_file ->     45	    for (i in 1:N) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     46	      for (j in 1:M) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     47	        real s = 0;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     48	        for (d in 1:D) {
[tool-d40] read_file ->     49	          real t = (X[i, d] - X_new[j, d]) / rho[d];
[tool-d40] read_file ->     50	          s += t * t;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     51	        }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     52	        Kx[i, j] = square(alpha) * exp(-0.5 * s);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     53	      }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     54	    }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     55	    return Kx;
[tool-d40] read_file ->     56	  }
[tool-d40] read_file ->     57	}

[tool-d40] read_file ->     89	  alpha ~ normal(0, 1.5);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     90	  rho   ~ lognormal(0, 1);

[tool-d40] read_file ->     92	  y ~ multi_normal_cholesky(mu, L_K);
[tool-d40] read_file ->     93	}
[tool-d40] read_file ->     94	
[tool-d40] read_file ->     95	generated quantities {

[tool-d40] read_file ->    104

[tool-d40] read_file ->    107	    vector[N_new] f_var = to_vector(diagonal(K_new)) - to_vector(columns_dot_self(v));
[tool-d40] read_file ->    108	    for (m in 1:N_new) {

[tool-d40] read_file ->    110	      real m_sd   = sqrt(f_var[m] + square(sigma));
[tool-d40] read_file ->    111	      y_new[m] = normal_rng(m_mean, m_sd);
[tool-d40] read_file ->    112	    }
[tool-d40] read_file ->    113	  }

## Summary

### 1. PyStan 3.10.0 installation (challenging on this ARM64 system)
This system is **Linux ARM64 (aarch64)**, and `httpstan` (PyStan's C++ backend) publishes **no aarch64 Linux wheels** on PyPI. I therefore:
- Downloaded the `httpstan 4.13.0` source from GitHub and patched its Makefile to fetch `linux-arm64-stanc` (ARM64 Stan compiler) instead of the x86_64 `linux-stanc`.
- Installed `g++`/`make` via apt, then built httpstan from source (downloaded Stan 2.35 / Math 4.9 / pybind11 / rapidjson, built sundials + TBB, compiled `stan_services.o`).
- Installed the built httpstan 4.13.0 plus `pystan 3.10.0`, `clikit`, and `pysimdjson`. Verified `stan.build(...)` compiles and samples correctly.

### 2. R script analysis
The model is a GP with ARD squared-exponential kernel, linear mean `H = [1, x1, x2]`, priors `beta~N(0,1)`, `alpha~N(0,1.5)`, `rho~lognormal(0,1)`, `sigma~N(0,0.5)`, with hyperparameters: 4 chains, `iter=2000, warmup=1000, thin=2`, `seed=1`, `init_r=0.1`, and adapt settings `delta=0.93, max_treedepth=14, gamma=0.05, kappa=0.75, t0=10, init_buffer=100, term_buffer=50, window=25`.

### 3. Conversion (`/app/pystan_analysis.py`)
- Loads the same CSV/JSON files and builds `H`/`H_new` exactly as the R script.
- Stan model is functionally identical. **One fix was required:** the original R script's generated-quantities block declares `matrix[N, N] v = mdivide_left_tri_low(L_K, K_x_xnew)`, but that expression is `[N, N_new]` (200×5), causing a runtime dimension error that would crash the original too. I corrected it to `matrix[N, N_new] v` (the GP predictive formula), leaving parameter sampling unaffected.
- Sampling hyperparameters mapped to PyStan equivalently (`num_warmup=1000, num_samples=1000, num_thin=2, init_radius=0.1, max_depth=14, delta=0.93, ...`) and `stan.build(..., random_seed=1)` as required.
- Also patched a latent bug in httpstan's `services_stub.py` error handler so underlying Stan errors are surfaced (doesn't affect results).

The full 4-chain run completed successfully and a re-run confirmed identical (deterministic, cached) posterior means.
